_source line 35_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I can see the `from_checkpoints` method (line 561). It uses `safetensors.torch.load_model()` directly which expects key names to match exactly. The problem is that pi0_libero has `model.` prefix on all keys.

I need to add a helper function that:
1. Detects if the checkpoint is LeRobot format (has `model.` prefix)
2. If so, strips the prefix and ignores normalization buffer keys
3. Loads the state_dict manually

Let me also look at how `safetensors.torch.load_model` works - it calls `model.load_state_dict` under the hood with the safetensors file. 

I'll create a custom loading function that handles the key mapping by loading the safetensors file directly, detecting and stripping the `model.` prefix if present, filtering out normalization-related keys, and then loading the cleaned state dict into the model.
```

</details>

Let me write a helper function and patch both `from_checkpoints` and `from_dummy` to use it.

  "command": "cd /root/meta_brain_lora_routing && cat > /tmp/patch_assembly.py << 'PYEOF'\nimport re\n\nwith open(\"phase1/meta_brain_assembly.py\", \"r\") as f:\n    content = f.read()\n\n# 1. Add helper function after _load_norm_stats function (before the class definition)\n# Find the end of _load_norm_stats function - look for the class definition or next function\nhelper_func = '''\ndef _load_pi0_checkpoint(model: nn.Module, checkpoint_dir: str) -> None:\n    \"\"\"Load PI0 checkpoint, handling both openpi and LeRobot formats.\"\"\"\n    ckpt_path = os.path.join(checkpoint_dir, \"model.safetensors\")\n    state_dict = safetensors.torch.load_file(ckpt_path)\n\n    # Detect LeRobot format: keys have 'model.' prefix\n    has_model_prefix = any(k.startswith(\"model.\") for k in state_dict)\n    if has_model_prefix:\n        # Strip 'model.' prefix and ignore normalization buffer keys\n        ignore_prefixes = (\"normalize_inputs.\", \"normalize_targets.\", \"unnormalize_outputs.\")\n        remapped = {}\n        ignored_count = 0\n        for k, v in state_dict.items():\n            if any(k.startswith(p) for p in ignore_prefixes):\n                ignored_count += 1\n                continue\n            if k.startswith(\"model.\"):\n                remapped[k[len(\"model.\"):]] = v\n            else:\n                remapped[k] = v\n        log.info(\"LeRobot format detected: stripped 'model.' prefix from %d keys, ignored %d normalization buffers\",\n                 len(remapped), ignored_count)\n        state_dict = remapped\n    else:\n        log.info(\"openpi format detected: %d keys\", len(state_dict))\n\n    missing, unexpected = model.load_state_dict(state_dict, strict=False)\n    if missing:\n        log.warning(\"Missing keys (%d): %s\", len(missing), missing[:5])\n    if unexpected:\n        log.warning(\"Unexpected keys (%d): %s\", len(unexpected), unexpected[:5])\n    if not missing and not unexpected:\n        log.info(\"All %d keys loaded successfully\", len(state_dict))\n\n'''\n\n# Insert the helper before the MetaBrain class definition\nclass_marker = \"\\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\\n# MetaBrain\"\nif class_marker not in content:\n    # Try finding class MetaBrain\n    class_marker = \"\\nclass MetaBrain\"\n\n# Find a good insertion point - after _load_norm_stats ends, before the class\n# Look for the pattern where MetaBrain class starts\ninsert_marker = \"# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\\n# MetaBrain\"\nif insert_marker in content:\n    content = content.replace(insert_marker, helper_func + insert_marker)\nelse:\n    # Fallback: insert before \"class MetaBrain\"\n    content = content.replace(\"\\nclass MetaBrain\", helper_func + \"\\nclass MetaBrain\")\n\n# 2. Replace the safetensors.torch.load_model calls in from_checkpoints and from_dummy\n# In from_checkpoints:\nold_load_1 = '''        safetensors.torch.load_model(\n            model, os.path.join(pi0_checkpoint, \"model.safetensors\")\n        )\n        model = model.to(device_t).eval()\n        log.info(\"PI0 loaded on %s\", device)\n\n        router = PARRouter.from_checkpoint'''\n\nnew_load_1 = '''        _load_pi0_checkpoint(model, pi0_checkpoint)\n        model = model.to(device_t).eval()\n        log.info(\"PI0 loaded on %s\", device)\n\n        router = PARRouter.from_checkpoint'''\n\ncontent = content.replace(old_load_1, new_load_1, 1)\n\n# In from_dummy:\nold_load_2 = '''        safetensors.torch.load_model(\n            model, os.path.join(pi0_checkpoint, \"model.safetensors\")\n        )\n        model = model.to(device_t).eval()\n        log.info(\"PI0 loaded on %s\", device)\n\n        # Dummy router'''\n\nnew_load_2 = '''        _load_pi0_checkpoint(model, pi0_checkpoint)\n        model = model.to(device_t).eval()\n        log.info(\"PI0 loaded on %s\", device)\n\n        # Dummy router'''\n\ncontent = content.replace(old_load_2, new_load_2, 1)\n\nwith open(\"phase1/meta_brain_assembly.py\", \"w\") as f:\n    f.write(content)\n\nprint(\"Patch applied successfully\")\n\n# Verify\nwith open(\"phase1/meta_brain_assembly.py\", \"r\") as f:\n    patched = f.read()\n\nassert \"_load_pi0_checkpoint\" in patched, \"Helper function not found!\"\nassert \"safetensors.torch.load_model\" not in patched, \"Old load_model calls still present!\"\nprint(\"Verification passed: helper function present, old load_model calls removed\")\nPYEOF\npython /tmp/patch_assembly.py\n",
  "account": "root"
}

indent=2)\\n    log.info(\\\"Results saved to %s\\\", out_path)\\n\\n    latex_row = results_to_latex(results, args.routing)\\n    log.info(\\\"LaTeX row: %s\\\", latex_row)\\n\\n _source line 96_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
So `embed_tokens` is not in the base checkpoint either. It's a tied weight in the PI0Pytorch model that is initialized from `lm_head.weight` at model init time. The PI0Pytorch model creates the `embed_tokens` module but its weight is tied to `lm_head.weight`. Since neither base nor libero store it separately, it must be handled by the model itself during initialization (weight tying).

So actually the missing key issue is benign - both base and libero checkpoints don't have this key. The `safetensors.torch.load_model` used before might have been handling it silently, or PI0Pytorch uses weight tying. 

Since I'm using `strict=False`, the missing key warning is just informational and the model will work fine with the tied weight. The original code loaded pi0_base with `safetensors.torch.load_model` which likely handles tied weights differently than `model.load_state_dict`, probably by skipping keys that don't exist in the checkpoint. I think the key difference is how `safetensors.torch.load_model` handles tied weights compared to PyTorch's `load_state_dict`. With tied weights, the model's state dict has both keys pointing to the same tensor, but safetensors only stores one copy in the file. When loading, safetensors iterates through the file's keys and assigns them directly to the model, which means it only needs the one key that's actually stored—the other tied key gets updated automatically since they share the same tensor in memory.

But when `load_state_dict` is called, it expects both keys to be present and creates separate tensors for each, which breaks the tie. So the issue is that loading `lm_head.weight` creates a new tensor while `embed_tokens.weight` stays pointing to the old initialized values. Let me check where exactly the lm_head is positioned in this model's architecture to understand the tie better.

Looking at the shapes, both `embed_tokens.weight` and `lm_head.weight` should be (257152, 2048), so they're meant to be the same tensor. After loading the checkpoint, I'll need to manually re-tie them. Though it's odd that pi0_base worked fine without `embed_tokens` at all—maybe the model doesn't actually reference it during inference, or it only uses it in certain code paths.

The quickest approach is to copy the `lm_head` weight to `embed_tokens` after loading with `strict=False`. But first, let me focus on fixing the router crash since that's the immediate blocker—I can verify whether inference works once that's resolved.
```

I need to fix `from_checkpoints()` to handle the case when `router_checkpoint` is empty - in that case, create a dummy router (same as from_dummy does).

Also, I need to handle the embed_tokens tied weight. Let me update `_load_pi0_checkpoint` to handle it.


Now I'm implementing the fixes for both the empty router checkpoint case and the tied weight handling.

Both episodes failed. This is concerning - if pi0_libero should get ~96.85% on LIBERO nominal, but we're getting 0% so far, something might be wrong with the normalization or the model's action generation.

Let me think about what could be wrong:
1. The model loaded correctly (777 keys matched)
2. Norm stats loaded from the checkpoint dir
3. But the evaluation uses `from_dummy` (since no lora_dirs), not `from_checkpoints`

Wait - looking at the eval script more carefully:
```python
if lora_dirs:
    mb = MetaBrain.from_checkpoints(...)


```

Since we're passing no LoRA dirs, it uses `from_dummy` instead, which creates dummy LoRA banks with zero B matrices and no correction applied. With routing mode set to 'none', the predict_action should just use the frozen model output directly. But I need to verify that the predict_action path with `routing_mode='none'` actually skips the LoRA application correctly.

Looking at the dry run output showing `Action shape: torch.Size([1, 50, 32]), range: [-5.929, 4.114]`, this is the unnormalized action. The real issue is that LIBERO expects 7D actions (6 joints plus gripper), but the model is outputting 32D actions that need proper mapping. I should trace through how the evaluate function handles observation normalization and action unnormalization to see where the mismatch occurs.

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"def run_episode(\\n    meta_brain,\\n    task_suite_name,\\n    perturbation_type,\\n    seed,\\n    max_steps=None,\\n):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Run one LIBERO-Plus episode with MetaBrain.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    if max_steps is None:\\n        max_steps = MAX_STEPS.get(task_suite_name, 400)\\n\\n    env, task_description, init_states, task_idx, suite_key = create_libero_env(\\n        task_suite_name, perturbation_type, seed,\\n    )\\n\\n    import random\\n    trial_idx = random.randint(0, len(init_states) - 1)\\n\\n    step = 0\\n    success = False\\n    episode_routing = []\\n\\n    try:\\n        obs = env.set_init_state(init_states[trial_idx])\\n        for _ in range(NUM_STEPS_WAIT):\\n            obs, _, _, _ = env.step(LIBERO_DUMMY_ACTION)\\n\\n        action_plan = collections.deque()\\n        done = False\\n\\n        while not done and step < max_steps:\\n            if len(action_plan) == 0:\\n                obs_dict = obs_to_model_input(obs, task_description)\\n                result = meta_brain.infer(obs_dict)\\n                action_chunk = result[\\\"actions\\\"]\\n                action_plan.extend(action_chunk[:REPLAN_STEPS])\\n                if \\\"routing_weights\\\" in result:\\n                    episode_routing.append(result[\\\"routing_weights\\\"])\\n\\n            action = action_plan.popleft()\\n            if hasattr(action, \\\"tolist\\\"):\\n                action = action.tolist()\\n            obs, reward, done, info = env.step(action)\\n            step += 1\\n\\n            if done or reward > 0:\\n                success = info.get(\\\"success\\\", reward > 0)\\n    finally:\\n        env.close()\\n\\n    return {\\n        \\\"success\\\": success,\\n        \\\"steps\\\": step,\\n        \\\"task_description\\\": task_description,\\n        \\\"task_idx\\\": task_idx,\\n        \\\"suite_key\\\": suite_key,\\n        \\\"perturbation_type\\\": perturbation_type or \\\"nominal\\\",\\n        \\\"routing_weights_mean\\\": (\\n            np.mean(episode_routing, axis=0).tolist() if episode_routing else None\\n        ),\\n    }\\n\\n\\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\\n# Evaluation loop\\n# Parse images to uint8 HWC\\n        def _parse_image(img):\\n            img = np.asarray(img)\\n            if np.issubdtype(img.dtype, np.floating):\\n                img = (255 * np.clip(img, 0, 1)).astype(np.uint8)\\n            if img.ndim == 3 and img.shape[0] == 3:\\n                img = einops.rearrange(img, \\\"c h w -> h w c\\\")\\n            return img\\n\\n        base_image = _parse_image(obs_dict[\\\"observation/image\\\"])\\n        wrist_image = _parse_image(obs_dict[\\\"observation/wrist_image\\\"])\\n\\n        inputs = {\\n            \\\"state\\\": obs_dict[\\\"observation/state\\\"],\\n            \\\"image\\\": {\\n                \\\"base_0_rgb\\\": base_image,\\n                \\\"left_wrist_0_rgb\\\": wrist_image,\\n                \\\"right_wrist_0_rgb\\\": np.zeros_like(base_image),\\n            },\\n            \\\"image_mask\\\": {\\n                \\\"base_0_rgb\\\": np.True_,\\n                \\\"left_wrist_0_rgb\\\": np.True_,\\n                \\\"right_wrist_0_rgb\\\": np.False_,\\n            },\\n        }\\n\\n        if \\\"prompt\\\" in obs_dict:\\n            inputs[\\\"prompt\\\"] = obs_dict[\\\"prompt\\\"]\\n\\n        # I can see the `infer` method now. Let me trace through the logic:

1. Takes observation, normalizes state, preprocesses images
2. Calls `predict_action(observation)` which returns 32D actions (raw model output)
3. Unnormalizes actions using `a_mean` and `a_std` (32D vectors)
4. Applies delta-to-absolute: `actions[:, :, :6] += raw_state[:, :6]`
5. Slices to 7DOF: `actions_7dof = actions_np[:, :7]`

This seems correct in principle. 

But I'm noticing a couple of potential issues here. The state is 8D (position, axis-angle, gripper), yet the normalization stats are 32D—only the first 8 dimensions are actually used. And I'm not entirely sure whether pi0 outputs normalized deltas or absolute actions, which would affect how the delta-to-absolute conversion should work.

Looking at the pi0 paper and how LeRobot trained it, pi0 outputs normalized action chunks based on training data statistics. For pi0_libero specifically, it was trained on absolute Cartesian end-effector actions. So if the model is already outputting absolute actions, then adding the current state position would be incorrect. Unless—pi0_libero was actually trained to predict deltas instead, in which case the addition makes sense.

The real issue is figuring out what pi0_libero actually outputs after unnormalization, and whether LIBERO's step function expects delta or absolute actions. Let me trace through the code to see what the norm_stats reveal about the training data. I'm realizing the delta-to-absolute conversion might actually be correct for pi0 itself, since the baseline model was already getting 0% success—that was a known limitation. The real question is whether pi0_libero, which was fine-tuned on LIBERO data, will perform better, and if it also fails, then there's likely a fundamental action space mismatch. Let me just run the evaluation and check the logs to see what's actually happening.

What's striking is that the action orientation values are nearly the negatives of the state orientation values, suggesting the actions might represent corrections or reversals of the current orientation state.

Looking at the position components more closely, the z-axis values show an interesting pattern: the state z-coordinate is around 0.76 while the action z is around -0.80, which hints at some kind of compensatory or opposing movement, though the x and y components don't follow this exact negative relationship.

The action values appear to be in absolute Cartesian space rather than deltas, matching the scale of the state values. This suggests the model was trained to predict absolute EEF targets directly. However, there's a code path that adds the current state to the actions, which would double the actual target position if the actions are already absolute—that seems like a bug. Looking at how LIBERO's environment works, the OSC_POSE controller expects a 7D vector of deltas (position and orientation changes plus gripper command), not absolute poses. So if pi0 is predicting absolute positions, I need to convert them to deltas by subtracting the current EEF state. The current code that adds the state to the actions is doing the opposite of what's needed.

But wait—let me check what the model is actually trained to predict. The action mean values like [-2.97, 0.23, 0.12] don't look like typical delta values which should be near zero. And comparing against the state mean, the numbers seem to be in absolute position scale, which confirms the model outputs absolute poses. The fix is definitely to subtract the current state from predicted actions to get the deltas LIBERO expects. the action orientation is parameterized as the negative of the state orientation. Looking at the exact numerical opposites across dimensions 3-5 (state and action values are precise negatives of each other), this strongly suggests the model is learning to output orientation in an inverted representation compared to how it's stored in the state.

But wait—if these are deltas in the robot's base frame, the magnitudes don't make sense; a single-step delta wouldn't be that large. More likely, the actions represent absolute target poses for the end of each trajectory chunk, since the model predicts 50 action chunks. The action means would then reflect average absolute targets across training data, which explains why action_mean and state_mean have similar magnitudes but opposite signs for orientation.

The orientation sign flip suggests the action uses a different parameterization than the state—maybe a rotation from base to target or an inverted axis-angle convention. The real issue is that the current inference code adds state to action, which would double absolute targets instead of converting deltas. I need to check how the official OpenPI codebase handles LIBERO inference to understand the correct transformation.

Actually, LIBERO defaults to OSC_POSE with delta actions, not absolute. So the model predicts absolute target poses, but I need to convert them to deltas by subtracting the current state before sending to the environment. The current code is doing the opposite—adding instead of subtracting.

But wait, those action magnitudes seem too large for single-step deltas. They might be multi-step targets since the model has an action_horizon of 50, meaning each predicted action in the sequence is actually a target for that step ahead.

So the pi0 model outputs absolute EEF positions at future timesteps, and with REPLAN_STEPS=5, we only use the first 5 of those predicted positions. Since LIBERO expects deltas, I need to convert each predicted absolute position to a delta by subtracting the current EEF state. The tricky part is that the current state changes after each step, so ideally I'd compute delta_i = action_i - eef_state_after_step_(i-1), but the current implementation seems to be applying the conversion differently.

Looking at the execution loop, the code fetches a chunk of 50 actions, takes the first 5, and executes them one at a time while the robot state updates between steps. The infer() method already handles the delta conversion for all 50 actions in the chunk, but since we're only using 5 of them sequentially and the robot's state changes after each execution, there's a mismatch—the deltas were computed assuming a certain starting state, but by step 2 or 3, the robot has already moved and those pre-computed deltas are no longer correct relative to the new state.

The conversion logic adds the current state offset to all 50 action positions at once, treating them as if they're all relative to the same EEF position. ### Assistant

_source line 184_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I can see the reference implementation clearly. In the openpi reference:

1. The observation is sent to the model server via websocket
2. The model server handles normalization/unnormalization internally
3. The returned `action_chunk` is directly passed to `env.step(action.tolist())` - NO delta conversion!

So the openpi model internally handles:
- Input state normalization
- Action prediction
- Action unnormalization

And the output is in the format that LIBERO expects directly: the 7D actions are already in the delta format that the OSC controller expects. This means the pi0_libero model was trained with delta actions (relative to current state), not absolute positions!
 
The action_mean values like -2.97 make sense now that I understand the full pipeline: the server normalizes incoming states, the model predicts normalized actions, then the server unnormalizes them back using the action statistics before sending them to the environment. So those large mean values are part of the unnormalization process that converts the model's output into the actual deltas the controller expects.

Looking at my `infer()` method, I'm applying an extra delta-to-absolute conversion that shouldn't be there—the unnormalized actions are already in the correct format for the OSC controller and should go straight to `env.step()` without modification. The model was trained on raw LIBERO action values, so after unnormalization they're ready to use as-is.

But those action_mean values are puzzling me—seeing -2.97 for orientation components doesn't align with typical small deltas like 0.01 for OSC_POSE actions. This suggests the LIBERO training data might be using a different action representation than I assumed, so I need to check what the data conversion script actually does with the actions.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
OK so in the LIBERO data conversion, `step["action"]` is stored directly as the action. In the LIBERO dataset (from PI repo), the actions are in the format: `[eef_pos(3), eef_axisangle(3), gripper(1)]` — these are **absolute EEF targets**, not deltas.

Wait let me reconsider. Looking at the LIBERO codebase and how the demo data is recorded: the raw data stores `actions` which are what was sent to `env.step()`. In robosuite with the OSC_POSE controller, the actions sent to env.step() are **deltas** [Δx, Δy, Δz, Δax, Δay, Δaz, gripper].
 
But the norm_stats showing action_mean values like [-2.97, 0.23] for orientation components are way too large for step-level deltas — those would be order of 0.01 with mean near zero. This suggests the actions are actually absolute EEF targets, which aligns with how Physical Intelligence's pi0 model works: predicting absolute state targets at each timestep rather than deltas, so the norm stats reflect the distribution of absolute EEF poses across the dataset.

Looking at the actual numbers, the position components track reasonably well between state and action (state z ≈ 0.76, action z ≈ -0.81), but the orientations are negated (state[3] ≈ 2.97, action[3] ≈ -2.97), which would imply the robot is constantly trying to flip 180 degrees — that can't be right. There must be something about how LIBERO represents actions that I'm missing.

Actually, I think the actions represent absolute EEF poses at the next timestep rather than deltas, which explains why the statistics look similar. The orientation sign flip might stem from different axis-angle conventions or coordinate frame representations between how states and actions are stored in the teleoperated demos.

Looking at the position statistics more carefully, the z-coordinates have opposite signs (state z ≈ 0.76 vs action z ≈ -0.81), which is puzzling if both are supposed to be absolute positions in the same frame. This suggests either a coordinate frame mismatch or that the action representation is fundamentally different from what I initially thought.

Rather than getting stuck on the dataset semantics, I should follow what the openpi reference implementation actually does: it unnormalizes the model output and sends it directly to env.step() without any delta-to-absolute conversion. Our code has that conversion step which is likely wrong, so I need to remove it.
```

{
  "server": "westd-34142",
  "command": "source /root/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh && conda activate base && cd /root/meta_brain_lora_routing && python3 << 'EOF'\ncontent = open('phase1/meta_brain_assembly.py').read()\n\n# Remove the delta-to-absolute conversion - it's wrong per openpi reference\nold_delta = '''        # Delta-to-absolute: actions[:, :, :6] += raw_state[:, :6]\n        actions[:, :, :6] = actions[:, :, :6] + raw_state[:, :6].unsqueeze(1).to(actions.dtype)\n\n        # Slice to LIBERO action dim (7) and remove batch dim'''\n\nnew_delta = '''        # Slice to LIBERO action dim (7) and remove batch dim'''\n\nassert old_delta in content, f\"delta-to-absolute pattern not found!\"\ncontent = content.replace(old_delta, new_delta, 1)\n\n# Also remove the raw_state save line since it's no longer used\nold_raw = '''        # Save raw 8D state for delta-to-absolute conversion\n        raw_state = inputs[\"state\"].clone()  # [1, 8]\n\n        # Normalize state (8D) before padding'''\n\nnew_raw = '''        # Normalize state (8D) before padding'''\n\nassert old_raw in content, \"raw_state pattern not found!\"\ncontent = content.replace(old_raw, new_raw, 1)\n\nwith open('phase1/meta_brain_assembly.py', 'w') as f:\n    f.write(content)\n\n# Verify\npatched = open('phase1/meta_brain_assembly.py').read()\nassert 'raw_state' not in patched, \"raw_state still present!\"\nassert 'Delta-to-absolute' not in patched, \"delta-to-absolute comment still present!\"\nprint(\"Delta-to-absolute conversion removed successfully\")\nEOF\n",
  "account": "root"
}

```text
Episode 1 still failed (FAIL). The issue might not be the delta-to-absolute conversion alone. Let me think about what else could be wrong.

Looking at the openpi reference inference flow more carefully:
1. The client sends raw observations to the server
2. The server's normalization pipeline normalizes state, runs model, unnormalizes output
3. Output goes directly to env.step()

In our MetaBrain.infer():
1. We normalize state manually: `(state - state_mean[:8]) / (state_std[:8] + 1e-6)`


2. We pad state to 32D in `_preprocess_real_obs`
3. We run the model
4. We unnormalize: `actions = actions * (a_std + 1e-6) + a_mean`
5. We slice to 7D

The real problem could be that our normalization and unnormalization formulas don't match what openpi actually uses—maybe they're using quantile-based clipping instead of mean/std, or the model pipeline itself has some preprocessing step we're missing.

Let me dig into how openpi handles state normalization and action unnormalization, since that's likely where the discrepancy is.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

````text
I found a critical piece - the `DeltaActions` transform! OpenPi has a `DeltaActions` transform that converts absolute actions into delta actions during TRAINING:

```python
class DeltaActions(DataTransformFn):
    """Repacks absolute actions into delta action space."""
    mask: Sequence[bool] | None

    def __call__(self, data: DataDict) -> DataDict:
        if "actions" not in data or self.mask is None:
            return data
        state, actions = data["state"], data["actions"]
        mask = np.asarray(self.mask)


```

Continuing with the transform logic...

The transform subtracts the current state values from the actions to convert them to deltas, using the mask to determine which dimensions should be treated as relative. There should be a corresponding `UndeltaActions` transform for inference that reverses this operation. I also notice the `Unnormalize._unnormalize` function that scales values back using mean and standard deviation statistics.

The critical question is whether pi0_libero actually uses `DeltaActions` during training. If it does, the model predicts action deltas rather than absolute actions, which means I'd need to add the state back at inference time. But looking at the openpi reference implementation, it sends the model output directly to the environment without any delta conversion, which suggests either `DeltaActions` isn't being used for pi0_libero, or the openpi server handles that conversion internally. I should check the pi0_libero config to clarify whether `DeltaActions` is actually applied.
````

So for `pi0_libero` with `extra_delta_transform=True`:


During training, the model learns to predict action deltas for the first 6 dimensions (position and orientation) by subtracting the current state from the action, while keeping the gripper dimension unchanged. At inference time, the absolute action transform reverses this by adding the state back, so the pi0_libero model ultimately predicts relative movements.

But I'm realizing there's a conceptual issue here—LIBERO actions are already deltas, so subtracting the absolute state from these delta actions creates something like "delta minus absolute pose," which doesn't quite make sense semantically.

Looking at the normalization statistics though, the action mean for orientation is -2.97 while the state mean is 2.97, which is the opposite sign. This suggests that during training, the model is learning to predict the difference between raw delta actions and current state values, and the negative mean reflects this relationship. So the inference pipeline actually works out: the model predicts this normalized difference, we unnormalize it back to get the raw delta action minus state, then add the state back to recover the original delta actions that LIBERO expects.

Wait, that means my earlier "fix" of removing the delta-to-absolute conversion was actually wrong. I need to restore that addition step. But here's the puzzle: the original code with that conversion still got 0% success in the first run, so the conversion itself isn't the culprit. Something else must be broken.

Let me trace through what else could be causing the failure. The state input is only 8 dimensions but the model expects 32, so there's padding happening somewhere in the preprocessing. I should check how that's being handled.

Actually, I'm realizing I need to understand the exact order of operations in the OpenPI pipeline. The training config stacks transforms in one order—`LiberoInputs` and `DeltaActions` first, then normalization—but at inference time the pipeline reverses: we apply the input transform, normalize, run the model, then unnormalize and apply the inverse transforms. So the question is whether the delta-to-absolute conversion happens before or after the normalization step, because that could completely change the values.

Looking at our implementation, we're normalizing the state first, then running the model, then unnormalizing the action output before converting it to absolute actions by adding the raw unnormalized state. That matches what OpenPI does—the `AbsoluteActions` transform adds the raw state to the raw action output. But I need to verify this is actually the right order and that we're not accidentally mixing normalized and unnormalized values somewhere.
